3

6 
A.

7

8 
to be unrealistic load growth assumptions, an improper 
9 
change in forecasting methodology, and the lack of any 
10 
detailed plan to actually complete the projected capital 
11 
spending as proposed.” Mr. Madsen encourages the Commission 
12 
“… to approve an overall level of capital for the Company 
13 
that is realistic, achievable, and most importantly, no 
14

15 
safe and reliable electric and gas distribution system.”   
16

17 
has not incorporated recent changes in Federal policy on 
18

19 
deferred projects that could potentially be impacted by 
20 
delays in development of offshore wind. NYC EIP also 
21 
recommends that, “… unless the total amounts are

Case No. 25-E-0072

15 
• Recommendations based on uncertainty in estimated 
1 
project costs 
2 
• Rejection or reduction because specific work scopes 
3 
and timelines not available 
4 
Q. 
Please start by discussing Staff’s general reliance on past 
5 
budgeted and actual spend as proxy for future spend. 
6 
A. 
In many cases, Staff bases a proposed reduction on the 
7 
amount budgeted and/or actually spent on a program as a 
8 
guide to what future budgets should be. In some cases, 
9 
Staff cites a presumed inability for the Company to 
10 
complete an increased volume of work for a particular 
11 
program in a given year. 
12 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with this approach to determining 
13 
future budgets? 
14 
A. 
No. It is arbitrary to propose reductions to a specific 
15 
program without first evaluating the program and explaining 
16 
why the proposed reductions are justified in light of the 
17 
program’s activities. Reasoned decision-making requires 
18 
analysis of the Company’s reasons for its funding requests 
19 
and an explanation, supported by evidence, why those 
20 
reasons are incorrect or inadequate. Absent that, a 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072 
 
 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
 
ELECTRIC INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
REDACTED VERSION 
 
 
16 
proposal to cut funding based solely on the fact that the 
1 
Company has not historically reached that spending level is 
2 
simply a “hair-cut” that may not reflect system needs and 
3 
is unsupported by the record.  
4 
Relying on historic actual spending to forecast future 
5 
funding ignores the reality of changing economic and 
6 
operational conditions. Costs are not static and change due 
7 
to inflation, supply chain disruptions, regulatory 
8 
requirements and system conditions. For example, recent 
9 
years have seen significant increases in labor costs driven 
10 
by wage inflation and skilled workforce shortages. 
11 
Similarly, the cost of materials such as transformers and 
12 
conductors have risen sharply due to global supply 
13 
constraints and increased demand. Historical spending does 
14 
not always capture what will be required in the future. 
15 
Finally, the volume of work conducted in the past may be 
16 
based on a variety of factors and should not be interpreted 
17 
as a limiting factor to what the Company is capable of 
18 
achieving in the future. 
19 
Q. 
Do you any other concerns related to Staff’s use of 
20 
historical spend to determine future budgets? 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072 
 
 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
 
ELECTRIC INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
REDACTED VERSION 
 
 
17 
A. 
Yes. Staff switches between using a three-year historic 
1 
average and a five-year historical average, often without 
2 
justifying why it used one over the other. This is 
3 
particularly concerning for the five-year average because 
4 
two of the years in the five-year average were impacted by 
5 
the COVID pandemic. Because Staff often relies solely on 
6 
historic spending as the basis for a proposed reduction, it 
7 
fails to address the Company’s justifications, and the 
8 
reliability and resilience impacts that could result from 
9 
Staff’s proposed reductions. This results in Staff making 
10 
conclusory and unsupported proposals. 
11 
In summary, sole reliance on historic spending without 
12 
additional context is arbitrary and unreasonable. The 
13 
Company believes that projected future needs for capital 
14 
investment should be considered on their merit and in light 
15 
of a changing energy landscape and increased demand for 
16 
resiliency. The Company has provided detailed information 
17 
on the work it is forecasting for these programs in its 
18 
initial testimony and white papers, and that information is 
19 
unrefuted by Staff’s cuts based on historic numbers. 
20 
Q. 
Please continue by discussing Staff’s proposed reductions 
21 
based on historic underspend. 
22

A. 
In many cases, Staff bases a proposed adjustment on 
1 
differences between the Company’s historic budgets and its 
2 
actual historic spending. In particular, Staff identifies 
3

3

13

14

241 
Q. 
Do you agree with the Staff ERRPM Panel that the existing 
1 
NRI data shows no widespread reliability issues that would 
2 
warrant the extensive expansions proposed? 
3 
A. 
No, we do not. The Company disagrees with the Staff’s 
4 
assertion that the existing NRI data show no widespread 
5 
reliability concerns because it overlooks critical context. 
6 
The current NRI figures are based on Summer 2024 conditions 
7 
using an 86-degree Fahrenheit design temperature, yet the 
8 
system is expected to operate under significantly more 
9 
stressful conditions—ramping up to 87-degree Fahrenheit by 
10 
2030. If the same analysis were performed using a TV of 
11 
86.4-degree Fahrenheit (the next step in the ramp-up), the 
12 
NRI values would already be measurably higher. Furthermore, 
13 
forward-looking NRI analysis shows that by 2030, with 
14 
increased loads and higher TV assumptions, many networks 
15 
will see their reliability metrics degrade, reverting to 
16 
levels not seen since 2013. Another way to look at this is 
17 
to say that the amount of work done to improve the networks 
18 
NRI from 2013 to 2025 (12 years), must be 
19 
completed/achieved again in the five years from now until 
20 
Summer 2030.

--- Page 350 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072

2

3

4

5

6

7 
need for a deferral mechanism. If a wildfire were to cause 
8 
damage to the Company's system, the Company is obligated to 
9 
repair that damage to ensure safe and reliable service. 
10 
Without a deferral mechanism, the Company would then need 
11 
to include in its request adequate funds to address such an 
12 
event. The use of a deferral mechanism would only require 
13 
funding if such an event were to occur, and at the level of 
14 
funding equal to the actual costs. For a low risk, but 
15 
potentially costly event, a deferral mechanism is best for 
16 
all as the rate payer will not have to pay for an event 
17 
that does not occur, and such an event would not impact 
18 
other reliability work, beyond the restoration effort, if 
19 
such an event does occur.

A.

1

2

3

4

6

7 
as system topology and various loading scenarios. This 
8 
risk-based methodology enables the Company to maintain 
9 
system reliability, given the uncertainties related to the 
10 
clean energy transition and climate change.

123. In that interrogatory, NYECC requested the Company

10

12 
of energy delivered by its transmission system that is 
13

14

15 
NYECC-26-123 where the Company responded that it does not 
16

17 
Q.

18 
A. 
The resource mix that makes up the energy delivered through 
19 
Con Edison’s transmission system is determined by energy 
20 
markets managed by the New York Independent System Operator 
21 
(“NYISO”). These allow market participants to buy and sell 
22 


--- Page 392 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072 
 
 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
 
ELECTRIC INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
REDACTED VERSION 
 
 
391 
energy and ancillary services at prices established through 
1 
real-time and day-ahead auctions designed to meet demand 
2 
with the lowest cost resources available. NYISO’s markets 
3 
incent resource availability, and allow for constantly 
4 
changing operating conditions. Given their nature, the 
5 
Company is not able to provide a detailed accounting of the 
6 
source of the specific energy delivered by the Company’s 
7 
transmission system, or what exactly that mix will be in 
8 
the future. Once electricity enters the bulk transmission 
9 
system, it becomes indistinguishable from other sources. 
10 
The distinction as “clean” energy lies in how the 
11 
electricity was produced, not how it is delivered or 
12 
consumed.

19

20 
public right-of-way. According to the NYC policy Panel, Con 
21 
Edison “considers anything and everything located in the 
22 


--- Page 405 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072 
 
 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC.

public right-of-way to be temporary in nature because the 
1

3 
Q. 
Do you agree with the NYC Policy Panel’s characterization? 
4 
A. 
No, we do not. The NYC Policy Panel mischaracterizes the 
5 
distinction between permanent and temporary service under 
6 
the Electric Tariff. Pursuant to the Electric Tariff, Leaf 
7 
35, Rule 5.2.1, Con Edison will provide a single service 
8 
line to a building or premises, at no cost to the customer. 
9 
For these customers, Con Edison has reasonable assurances 
10 
that it will recover the costs of the service installation. 
11 
Alternatively, Rule 5.2.7, Temporary Service, is used for 
12 
non-permanent structures, where Con Edison does not have 
13 
reason to believe that the facilities installed for service 
14 
will be used for permanent supply. That often occurs with 
15 
facilities installed in the public right-of-way where the 
16 
municipal authorities' rights to remove the customer 
17 
equipment, and the short term of the customer’s underlying 
18 
agreement, do not provide Con Edison with certainty long-
19 
term service and cost recovery.

14. 
Potential for Winter Peaking System – NYECC 
1 
Q. 
What does NYECC state related to the Company being winter 
2 
peaking in mid-2030? 
3 
A. 
NYECC claims that because this is so far in the future, 
4 
there is no need to address it now. 
5 
Q. 
Do you agree? 
6 
A. 
No, we do not. As described in the Company’s testimony, the 
7 
Company’s planning process takes into account many factors. 
8 
One key factor is load. Generally speaking, the load is 
9 
growing, and the Company’s plans reflect this. It is not so 
10 
much a shift from summer peak to winter peak, as it is that 
11 
winter peak will overtake summer peak. In other words, the 
12 
winter peak is expected to be higher than current or 
13 
forecasted future summer peaks. It follows that all the 
14 
Company’s investments in the electric infrastructure will 
15 
make the system that much closer to the capacity needed to 
16 
deliver safe and reliable electricity when the peak load is 
17 
higher and in winter. These and any future needs of the 
18 
system are taken into account when planning system 
19 
investments. The infrastructure investments that the 
20 
Company makes now have implications long into the future, 
21 


--- Page 409 ---
Case No. 25-E-0072 
 
 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
 
ELECTRIC INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
REDACTED VERSION 
 
 
408 
so it is critical that any and all future needs of the 
1 
system are considered while planning and executing 
2 
infrastructure upgrade plans.  
3

Does this conclude your testimony?  
4 
A. 
Yes.
